### TL;DR

This review presents universal bounds on entropy production derived from coarse-grained trajectories in stochastic thermodynamics, offering model-free estimation methods for non-equilibrium systems.

### Motivation

Entropy production is central to non-equilibrium thermodynamics but is often difficult to measure in partially observed systems. The review aims to consolidate approaches that infer lower bounds from coarse-grained observables.

### Method

The paper reviews stochastic thermodynamics frameworks that use fluctuating coarse-grained trajectories, including states, currents, correlations, and waiting times, to derive model-free lower bounds on entropy production in both steady and time-dependent regimes.

### Result

It highlights progress in trajectory-level estimation, providing universal bounds that apply across various observables and regimes.

### Conclusion

The review underscores the utility of coarse-grained approaches for quantifying non-equilibrium behavior and suggests they are a powerful tool for experimental applications.

*Keywords: entropy production, stochastic thermodynamics, coarse-grained trajectories, fluctuation theorems, non-equilibrium*

This paper provides a complete theory of the Clifford commutant, offering explicit bases and a graphical calculus, with applications to magic measures and stabilizer property testing.

### Motivation

### Method

The authors derive an explicit orthogonal basis for the Clifford commutant for arbitrary tensor power and qubit number, introduce an alternative basis of isotropic sums of Pauli operators, and develop a graphical calculus for manipulation.

They compute the dimension of the commutant, show the basis is generated by permutations and at most three other operators, and apply the theory to characterize all measurable magic measures and identify optimal strategies for stabilizer property testing.

### Conclusion

### TL;DR

This paper introduces a partition function framework to estimate logical error rates in stabilizer codes, linking decoding success to statistical mechanics and demonstrating improved sample efficiency.

### Motivation

Accurate estimation of logical error rates is crucial for assessing quantum error correction performance, but existing methods are sample-inefficient, especially at low noise.

### Method

The authors define a ratio of partition functions corresponding to maximum likelihood decoding and compare it with an order probability for a probabilistic decoder. They analyze the toric code under bitflip noise via the Random Bond Ising Model and also consider the color code.

### Result

They show that the partition function ratio differs from the order probability, and that estimating via these probabilities is more sample-efficient than counting decoder failures. They also discuss the role of degeneracy and ensembling.

### Conclusion

To understand whether the property of quantum nonlocality without entanglement is robust across different state discrimination measures, which is fundamental to the theory of quantum information.

### Method

The authors construct families of six linearly independent, equally probable bipartite product states and analyze their optimal discrimination under LOCC for both minimum-error and unambiguous discrimination, extending to multipartite systems with numerical evidence.

The property of quantum nonlocality without entanglement is not invariant under the choice of discrimination measure, which has implications for understanding the limits of LOCC and the nature of quantum correlations.

### Method

The authors develop a complete algorithmic pipeline, including state preparation, quantum phase estimation, and measurement, tailored for the Jones polynomial. They implement it on a trapped-ion quantum computer and analyze the resource requirements and error mitigation strategies.

### Result

The implementation successfully approximates the Jones polynomial for small knots, demonstrating the feasibility of the approach. The paper also provides insights into the scaling and potential for quantum advantage in this problem.

### Conclusion

The work establishes the Jones polynomial as a promising benchmark for quantum computers, highlighting both the challenges and opportunities for achieving quantum advantage in topological quantum computation.

## 59. Dynamical Codes For Hardware With Noisy Readouts

- **Link**: [https://quantum-journal.org/papers/q-2026-07-29-2176/](https://quantum-journal.org/papers/q-2026-07-29-2176/)
- **Tags**: `noise`  `decoding`  `dynamical codes`  `measurement-biased noise`  `teraquop volume`

### TL;DR

This paper optimizes measurement schedules for dynamical color codes under measurement-biased noise, introducing the teraquop volume metric and showing that repeating measurements improves performance in measurement-biased regimes.

### Motivation

Dynamical stabilizer codes may enable large-scale quantum computation, but their performance depends on the measurement schedule, especially when measurements are noisy. The authors aim to optimize schedules for hardware with noisy readouts.

### Method

They analyze dynamically condensed color codes under various noise biases, focusing on measurement-biased noise. They introduce the teraquop volume (product of qubits and measurement rounds) and compare decoding strategies (belief matching vs. minimum-weight perfect matching).

### Result

Repeating measurements improves performance for measurement-biased noise, but offers little improvement for unbiased or X-biased noise. The teraquop volume reveals that differences are mainly due to measurement rounds, not qubit count. Belief matching can turn a worst-performing code into a best-performing one.

### Conclusion

The teraquop volume is a valuable metric for comparing codes, and optimizing measurement schedules is crucial for hardware with noisy readouts. Correlated error decoding is important for achieving best performance.

*Keywords: dynamical codes, measurement-biased noise, teraquop volume, belief matching, quantum error correction*

### Method

The authors improve upon prior work by Low, Kliuchnikov, and Schaeffer, providing a constructive algorithm that achieves the optimal asymptotic T-count using ancilla qubits, and also prove matching lower bounds.

### Result

They show that the optimal asymptotic T-count for approximating an arbitrary n-qubit state to error epsilon is O(4^n n) with ancilla, and similarly for diagonal unitaries. They also demonstrate that a tensor product of many single-qubit unitaries can be synthesized in parallel for the price of one.

### Conclusion

- **Tags**: `algorithms`  `bounds`  `graph states`  `Clifford circuits`  `CZ-complexity`

### TL;DR

This paper characterizes the minimum number of two-qubit Clifford operations (CZ-complexity) needed to prepare graph states, linking it to rank-width and providing efficient algorithms for interval and circle graphs.

### Motivation

Understanding the resource cost of preparing graph states is crucial for quantum information processing, as graph states are key resources for measurement-based quantum computing and quantum error correction.

### Method

The authors define CZ-complexity and prove a combinatorial characterization of graph-state transformations using vertex deletions, local complementations, and edge-complementations. They then relate CZ-complexity to rank-width and provide algorithms for specific graph classes.

### Result

They show that for any graph with n vertices and rank-width r, the CZ-complexity is O(n*r) and at least Ω(n*r) for connected graphs, with bounds close to optimal. They also give O(n) and O(n log n) algorithms for interval and circle graphs, respectively.

### Conclusion

The work provides a deeper understanding of the complexity of graph-state preparation, offering both theoretical bounds and practical algorithms, which may inform resource optimization in quantum information tasks.

*Keywords: graph states, Clifford circuits, CZ-complexity, rank-width, quantum circuit complexity*

### TL;DR

Introduces Shadow Pauli Flow, a polynomial-time computable necessary and sufficient condition for robust determinism in measurement-based quantum computing with Pauli measurements.

### Motivation

Pauli Flow was previously shown to be insufficient for characterizing determinism in MBQC with Pauli measurements, leaving a gap in the theory.

### Method

The authors define Shadow Pauli Flow, an extension of Pauli Flow, and prove that an MBQC is robustly deterministic iff its correction strategy is consistent with a Shadow Pauli flow.

### Result

They demonstrate that Pauli flow is necessary for robust determinism in a weaker sense, and that Shadow Pauli Flow is both necessary and sufficient, computable in polynomial time.

### Conclusion
